========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um… yeah, I’m here because I can’t really stop thinking about my face. Like, especially the left side. It looks wrong to me—my nose, my jaw, like they’re crooked or shifted or something.

It shows up really bad in ID photos, and on the camera for online classes too. I know cameras distort things sometimes—like in the CNC lab we were talking about calibration and how tiny offsets can mess up a whole cut, and I keep thinking it’s like that with my face, like something is just off by a little but everyone can see it.

People tell me they don’t notice anything, but I don’t really believe them. I feel like when I’m in the lab, people notice that before they notice my work.

Like the work doesn’t even matter because that’s the first thing they see.\n\nDoctor: Does your concern about the left side of your face looking crooked stay the same all the time, or are there times when it bothers you less?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty much there all the time, I guess. Like, maybe I can get distracted for a bit if I’m really focused on a program or setting up a machine, but then I’ll see myself on the online class camera or in an ID photo and it’s right back.\n\nIt’s the left side—my nose and jaw just look off to me, crooked. And even when people say they don’t see it, I’m in the CNC lab thinking they’re noticing that before anything I’m doing. Like before my work or whether I actually know what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your face is actually fine and you're just perceiving it differently?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe, I guess it’s possible, but it doesn’t feel that way when I look at myself. Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of what I think sometimes. Like maybe they don’t want to be rude or make me feel worse, so they just say they don’t see it.

But I came in because I can’t stop thinking about it — the left side of my face just looks off to me, especially my nose and jaw. It’s in every ID photo, and on the online class camera it’s like I can’t stop staring at it. And then I’ll be in the CNC lab trying to focus on a setup or a program, and instead I’m thinking everyone’s noticing my face before they notice my work.

It’s hard because even if they’re trying to be nice, it still feels like they can see it.

I mean… maybe there’s a possibility, yeah. I can say that sitting here. But then when I actually see myself, like in an ID photo or on the online class camera, it just doesn’t feel normal. The left side looks off to me—my nose and jaw look crooked—and I can’t stop seeing it.

It looks wrong to me—my nose, my jaw, like they’re crooked or shifted or something.\n\nIt shows up really bad in ID photos, and on the camera for online classes too. I know cameras distort things sometimes—like in the CNC lab we were talking about calibration and how tiny offsets can mess up a whole cut, and I keep thinking it’s like that with my face, like something is just off by a little but everyone can see it.\n\nPeople tell me they don’t notice anything, but I don’t really believe them. I feel like when I’m in the lab, people notice that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually is crooked, or is it just that you feel like it looks that way to you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I think it actually is crooked. Or at least, it looks so off to me that it’s hard to believe it’s just in my head. Like the left side of my face—my nose and my jaw—especially in ID photos or on the camera for online classes, it’s like it jumps out at me.\n\nAnd I know people say they don’t see it, but then I’m in the CNC lab and I’m trying to focus on the machine or the program, and I just feel like everyone’s noticing my face before they notice anything I’m doing. Like the work doesn’t even matter because that’s the first thing they see.\n\nDoctor: Does your concern about the left side of your face looking crooked stay the same all the time, or are there times when it bothers you less?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty much there all the time, I guess. Like, maybe I can get distracted for a bit if I’m really focused on a program or setting up a machine, but then I’ll see myself on the online class camera or in an ID photo and it’s right back.\n\nIt’s the left side—my nose and jaw just look off to me, crooked. And even when people say they don’t see it, I’m in the CNC lab thinking they’re noticing that before anything I’m doing. Like before my work or whether I actually know what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your face is actually fine and you're just perceiving it differently?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe, I guess it’s possible, but it doesn’t feel that way when I look at myself. Like I came in because I can’t stop thinking about the left side of my face looking off—my nose and jaw, especially in ID photos or on the camera during online classes.\n\nIt’s like when I’m in the CNC lab and a part is just a little misaligned, even if someone else says it’s fine, I keep seeing the offset. And with my face it’s… I don’t know, people tell me they don’t see it, but I still feel like everyone notices that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people can see that your face is crooked, even if they don't say anything about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I know they don’t usually say anything, and if I ask they’ll say they don’t see it, but I still feel like they can. Like the left side of my face, my nose and jaw, it looks crooked to me in every ID photo or when I’m on the online class camera.\n\nAnd then in the CNC lab, I’ll be standing there trying to focus on the work, and I’m thinking they’re probably noticing my face first before they notice whether the part is right or if my program is good. It’s hard to explain, but it feels really obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that others are intentionally not commenting on your face because they don't want to hurt your feelings, rather than them actually noticing something wrong?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of what I think sometimes. Like maybe they don’t want to be rude or make me feel worse, so they just say they don’t see it.\n\nBut I came in because I can’t stop thinking about it — the left side of my face just looks off to me, especially my nose and jaw. It’s in every ID photo, and on the online class camera it’s like I can’t stop staring at it. And then I’ll be in the CNC lab trying to focus on a setup or a program, and instead I’m thinking everyone’s noticing my face before they notice my work.\n\nIt’s hard because even if they’re trying to be nice, it still feels like they can see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your face is actually normal and that your perception of it being crooked is not based on how it really looks?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe there’s a possibility, yeah. I can say that sitting here. But then when I actually see myself, like in an ID photo or on the online class camera, it just doesn’t feel normal. The left side looks off to me—my nose and jaw look crooked—and I can’t stop seeing it.\n\nIt’s kind of like in the CNC lab when a setup looks slightly wrong and I keep checking it even if someone else says it’s within tolerance… sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked. But yeah, people tell me they don’t see it, and I try to believe them, but I still feel like everyone is noticing my face before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Does your idea of how crooked your face looks ever change, or is it always the same?\n\nPatient: It changes a little, I guess… like some days it looks worse depending on the photo or the camera angle. But it’s always kind of there. I came in because I just can’t stop thinking about the left side of my face looking off, like my nose and jaw are crooked.\n\nIt’s weird because in ID photos or on the online class camera, I’ll keep staring at it, and then even in the CNC lab I’m supposed to be thinking about the machine or the program, and my mind goes to, “They’re noticing my face before they notice my work.” Even when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like they do.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Yeah… it has. I mean, more than I probably want to admit. I’ve missed CNC labs and some shop shifts because I just couldn’t get myself out the door. Like the left side of my face, my nose and jaw, it looks crooked to me in every ID photo or when I’m on the online class camera.\n\nAnd then in the CNC lab, I’ll be standing there trying to focus on the work, and I’m thinking they’re probably noticing my face first before they notice whether the part is right or if my program is good. It’s hard to explain, but it feels really obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that others are intentionally not commenting on your face because they don't want to hurt your feelings, rather than them actually noticing something wrong?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of what I think sometimes. Like maybe they don’t want to be rude or make me feel worse, so they just say they don’t see it.\n\nBut I came in because I can’t stop thinking about it — the left side of my face just looks off to me, especially my nose and jaw. It’s in every ID photo, and on the online class camera it’s like I can’t stop staring at it. And then I’ll be in the CNC lab trying to focus on a setup or a program, and instead I’m thinking everyone’s noticing my face before they notice my work.\n\nIt’s hard because even if they’re trying to be nice, it still feels like they can see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your face is actually normal and that your perception of it being crooked is not based on how it really looks?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe there’s a possibility, yeah. I can say that sitting here. But then when I actually see myself, like in an ID photo or on the online class camera, it just doesn’t feel normal. The left side looks off to me—my nose and jaw look crooked—and I can’t stop seeing it.\n\nIt’s kind of like in the CNC lab when a setup looks slightly wrong and I keep checking it even if someone else says it’s within tolerance… sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked. But yeah, people tell me they don’t see it, and I try to believe them, but I still feel like everyone is noticing my face before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Does your idea of how crooked your face looks ever change, or is it always the same?\n\nPatient: It changes a little, I guess… like some days it looks worse depending on the photo or the camera angle. But it’s always kind of there. I came in because I just can’t stop thinking about the left side of my face looking off, like my nose and jaw are crooked.\n\nIt’s weird because in ID photos or on the online class camera, I’ll keep staring at it, and then even in the CNC lab I’m supposed to be thinking about the machine or the program, and my mind goes to, “They’re noticing my face before they notice my work.” Even when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like they do.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the appearance of your face affected your relationships or daily activities in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, more than I probably want to admit. I’ve missed CNC labs and some shop shifts because I just couldn’t get myself out the door. I’d be ready, or almost ready, and then I’d catch my face in the mirror or think about the lights in the shop and everyone seeing me, and then suddenly I’m just stuck.\n\nAnd now my faculty mentor said my work placement could be in jeopardy, which… I know sounds serious. It is serious. I keep thinking I should be focusing on programming and setups and all that, because that’s what I’m actually there for, but instead I’m worried they’re looking at my face.\n\nI also stopped playing soccer and volunteering. I used to like that stuff, especially soccer, but now it feels like too many people looking at me from all angles, so I just don’t go.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah, definitely. I’ve stopped going to a lot of things. Like soccer, I just don’t play anymore, and I used to actually like it. Same with volunteering—I kind of just dropped it because I don’t want people seeing my face from every angle.

I feel like when I’m in the lab, people notice that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually is crooked, or is it just that you feel like it looks that way to you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I think it actually is crooked. And even when people say they don’t see it, I’m in the CNC lab thinking they’re noticing that before anything I’m doing. Like before my work or whether I actually know what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your face is actually fine and you're just perceiving it differently?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe, I guess it’s possible, but it doesn’t feel that way when I look at myself. Like I came in because I can’t stop thinking about the left side of my face looking off—my nose and jaw, especially in ID photos or on the camera during online classes.\n\nIt’s like when I’m in the CNC lab and a part is just a little misaligned, even if someone else says it’s fine, I keep seeing the offset. And with my face it’s… I don’t know, people tell me they don’t see it, but I still feel like everyone notices that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people can see that your face is crooked, even if they don't say anything about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Like the left side of my face, my nose and jaw, it looks crooked to me in every ID photo or when I’m on the online class camera.\n\nAnd then in the CNC lab, I’ll be standing there trying to focus on the work, and I’m thinking they’re probably noticing my face first before they notice whether the part is right or if my program is good. I came in because I just can’t stop thinking about the left side of my face looking off, like my nose and jaw are crooked.\n\nIt’s weird because in ID photos or on the online class camera, I’ll keep staring at it, and then even in the CNC lab I’m supposed to be thinking about the machine or the program, and my mind goes to, “They’re noticing my face before they notice my work.” Even when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like they do.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the appearance of your face affected your relationships or daily activities in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I can say that sitting here. But then when I actually see myself, like in an ID photo or on the online class camera, it just doesn’t feel normal. The left side looks off to me—my nose and jaw look crooked—and I can’t stop seeing it.\n\nIt’s kind of like in the CNC lab when a setup looks slightly wrong and I keep checking it even if someone else says it’s within tolerance… sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked. But yeah, people tell me they don’t see it, and I try to believe them, but I still feel like everyone is noticing my face before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Does your idea of how crooked your face looks ever change, or is it always the same?\n\nPatient: It changes a little, I guess… like some days it looks worse depending on the photo or the camera angle. But it’s always kind of there. I came in because I just can’t stop thinking about the left side of my face looking off, like my nose and jaw are crooked.\n\nIt’s weird because in ID photos or on the online class camera, I’ll keep staring at it, and then even in the CNC lab I’m supposed to be thinking about the machine or the program, and my mind goes to, “They’re noticing my face before they notice my work.” Even when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like they do.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the appearance of your face affected your relationships or daily activities in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, more than I probably want to admit. I’ve missed CNC labs and some shop shifts because I just couldn’t get myself out the door. I’d be ready, or almost ready, and then I’d catch my face in the mirror or think about the lights in the shop and everyone seeing me, and then suddenly I’m just stuck.\n\nAnd now my faculty mentor said my work placement could be in jeopardy, which… I know sounds serious. It is serious. I keep thinking I should be focusing on programming and setups and all that, because that’s what I’m actually there for, but instead I’m worried they’re looking at my face.\n\nI also stopped playing soccer and volunteering. I used to like that stuff, especially soccer, but now it feels like too many people looking at me from all angles, so I just don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social situations because you're worried about how people might see your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I’ve stopped going to a lot of things. Like soccer, I just don’t play anymore, and I used to actually like it. Same with volunteering—I kind of just dropped it because I don’t want people seeing my face from every angle.\n\nAnd it’s not just social stuff, I guess. I’ve missed CNC labs and shop shifts too because I’ll be trying to leave and then I get stuck thinking about how I look, and I can’t make myself go. My mentor even said my work placement could be in jeopardy, which makes me feel worse, but then that almost makes it harder to show up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact on your daily life or overall well-being due to your concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s kind of taken over more than I thought it would. Like, I’ve missed CNC labs and shop shifts because I’ll get ready and then just can’t get myself out the door. And now my faculty mentor said my work placement might be in jeopardy, which is… I mean, that’s a big deal.\n\nAnd then outside of school or work, I stopped playing soccer and volunteering. I used to like having something to do, you know, like being part of something, but now I just think about people looking at me, especially from the side, and I end up not going. Like the work doesn’t even matter because that’s the first thing they see.\n\nDoctor: Does your concern about the left side of your face looking crooked stay the same all the time, or are there times when it bothers you less?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty much there all the time, I guess. Like, maybe I can get distracted for a bit if I’m really focused on a program or setting up a machine, but then I’ll see myself on the online class camera or in an ID photo and it’s right back.\n\nIt’s the left side—my nose and jaw just look off to me, crooked. And even when people say they don’t see it, I’m in the CNC lab thinking they’re noticing that before anything I’m doing. Like before my work or whether I actually know what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your face is actually fine and you're just perceiving it differently?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe, I guess it’s possible, but it doesn’t feel that way when I look at myself. Like I came in because I can’t stop thinking about the left side of my face looking off—my nose and jaw, especially in ID photos or on the camera during online classes.\n\nIt’s like when I’m in the CNC lab and a part is just a little misaligned, even if someone else says it’s fine, I keep seeing the offset. And with my face it’s… I don’t know, people tell me they don’t see it, but I still feel like everyone notices that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people can see that your face is crooked, even if they don't say anything about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I know they don’t usually say anything, and if I ask they’ll say they don’t see it, but I still feel like they can. Like the left side of my face, my nose and jaw, it looks crooked to me in every ID photo or when I’m on the online class camera.\n\nAnd then in the CNC lab, I’ll be standing there trying to focus on the work, and I’m thinking they’re probably noticing my face first before they notice whether the part is right or if my program is good. It’s hard to explain, but it feels really obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that others are intentionally not commenting on your face because they don't want to hurt your feelings, rather than them actually noticing something wrong?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of what I think sometimes. Like maybe they don’t want to be rude or make me feel worse, so they just say they don’t see it.\n\nBut I came in because I can’t stop thinking about it — the left side of my face just looks off to me, especially my nose and jaw. It’s in every ID photo, and on the online class camera it’s like I can’t stop staring at it. And then I’ll be in the CNC lab trying to focus on a setup or a program, and instead I’m thinking everyone’s noticing my face before they notice my work.\n\nIt’s hard because even if they’re trying to be nice, it still feels like they can see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your face is actually normal and that your perception of it being crooked is not based on how it really looks?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe there’s a possibility, yeah. I can say that sitting here. But then when I actually see myself, like in an ID photo or on the online class camera, it just doesn’t feel normal. The left side looks off to me—my nose and jaw look crooked—and I can’t stop seeing it.\n\nIt’s kind of like in the CNC lab when a setup looks slightly wrong and I keep checking it even if someone else says it’s within tolerance… sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked. But yeah, people tell me they don’t see it, and I try to believe them, but I still feel like everyone is noticing my face before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Does your idea of how crooked your face looks ever change, or is it always the same?\n\nPatient: It changes a little, I guess… like some days it looks worse depending on the photo or the camera angle. But it’s always kind of there. I came in because I just can’t stop thinking about the left side of my face looking off, like my nose and jaw are crooked.\n\nIt’s weird because in ID photos or on the online class camera, I’ll keep staring at it, and then even in the CNC lab I’m supposed to be thinking about the machine or the program, and my mind goes to, “They’re noticing my face before they notice my work.” Even when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like they do.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the appearance of your face affected your relationships or daily activities in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, more than I probably want to admit. I’ve missed CNC labs and some shop shifts because I just couldn’t get myself out the door. I’d be ready, or almost ready, and then I’d catch my face in the mirror or think about the lights in the shop and everyone seeing me, and then suddenly I’m just stuck.\n\nAnd now my faculty mentor said my work placement could be in jeopardy, which… I know sounds serious. It is serious. I keep thinking I should be focusing on programming and setups and all that, because that’s what I’m actually there for, but instead I’m worried they’re looking at my face.\n\nI also stopped playing soccer and volunteering. I used to like that stuff, especially soccer, but now it feels like too many people looking at me from all angles, so I just don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social situations because you're worried about how people might see your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I’ve stopped going to a lot of things. Like soccer, I just don’t play anymore, and I used to actually like it. Same with volunteering—I kind of just dropped it because I don’t want people seeing my face from every angle.\n\nAnd it’s not just social stuff, I guess. I’ve missed CNC labs and shop shifts too because I’ll be trying to leave and then I get stuck thinking about how I look, and I can’t make myself go. My mentor even said my work placement could be in jeopardy, which makes me feel worse, but then that almost makes it harder to show up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact on your daily life or overall well-being due to your concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s kind of taken over more than I thought it would. Like, I’ve missed CNC labs and shop shifts because I’ll get ready and then just can’t get myself out the door. And now my faculty mentor said my work placement might be in jeopardy, which is… I mean, that’s a big deal.\n\nAnd then outside of school or work, I stopped playing soccer and volunteering. I used to like having something to do, you know, like being part of something, but now I just think about people looking at me, especially from the side, and I end up not going. It’s annoying because I know I should be worried about normal stuff, like finishing my program, but it always comes back to my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked is based on reality or is it something that you just can't shake no matter what others tell you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I want to say it’s based on reality because I can see it. Like in every ID photo, or when the online class camera flips or doesn’t flip, I don’t even know which one is worse, but the left side of my face just looks off. My nose and jaw look crooked to me.\n\nBut then people say they don’t see it, or they act like I’m making a big deal out of nothing, and I don’t know… I still can’t shake it. I’ll be in the CNC lab trying to focus on the setup or whatever, and I just feel like everyone notices my face before they notice anything I’m doing. So even if they say they don’t see it, it doesn’t really make it go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked would change if you saw a picture of yourself that someone else had taken and they told you you looked fine in it?\n\nPatient: I mean… probably not, honestly. I’d want it to, but if someone else took the picture and said I looked fine, I’d still zoom in or compare sides or think maybe it was just the angle.\n\nIt’s like, I came in because I can’t stop thinking the left side of my face looks off—my nose, my jaw, all of it. It shows up to me in ID photos, and then with online class cameras I get stuck wondering if it’s flipped or not flipped, and then I’m thinking about that instead of the class. Even in the CNC lab, I feel like people notice my face before they notice my work, even if they tell me they don’t see anything. So… yeah, one picture probably wouldn’t really change it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

So… yeah, one picture probably wouldn’t really change it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think there's anything that could convince you that your face is not actually crooked?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 20 / computer_numerically_controlled_tool_operator_or_programmer / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Preoccupation_with_Appearance\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I can’t stop thinking that the left side of my face looks off, like my nose and jaw are crooked in every ID photo or online class camera, and even when people say they don’t see it I feel like everyone in the CNC lab is noticing it before they notice my work.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know… I keep thinking maybe if there was some perfect angle or like, I don’t know, a scan or something, but then I’d probably still find a way to question it.

Because I came in because I just can’t stop thinking the left side of my face looks off. Like my nose and jaw look crooked to me in ID photos, and then with the online class camera I get stuck on whether it’s flipped or not, and I’ll be thinking about that instead of what we’re supposed to be doing. Even in the CNC lab, I feel like people notice my face before they notice my work.

So if people just say, “No, you look fine,” it doesn’t really convince me. I wish it did, but it just doesn’t stick.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, I’m here because I can’t really stop thinking about my face. Like, especially the left side. It looks wrong to me—my nose, my jaw, like they’re crooked or shifted or something.\n\nIt shows up really bad in ID photos, and on the camera for online classes too. I know cameras distort things sometimes—like in the CNC lab we were talking about calibration and how tiny offsets can mess up a whole cut, and I keep thinking it’s like that with my face, like something is just off by a little but everyone can see it.\n\nPeople tell me they don’t notice anything, but I don’t really believe them. I feel like when I’m in the lab, people notice that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually is crooked, or is it just that you feel like it looks that way to you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I think it actually is crooked. Or at least, it looks so off to me that it’s hard to believe it’s just in my head. Like the left side of my face—my nose and my jaw—especially in ID photos or on the camera for online classes, it’s like it jumps out at me.\n\nAnd I know people say they don’t see it, but then I’m in the CNC lab and I’m trying to focus on the machine or the program, and I just feel like everyone’s noticing my face before they notice anything I’m doing. Like the work doesn’t even matter because that’s the first thing they see.\n\nDoctor: Does your concern about the left side of your face looking crooked stay the same all the time, or are there times when it bothers you less?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty much there all the time, I guess. Like, maybe I can get distracted for a bit if I’m really focused on a program or setting up a machine, but then I’ll see myself on the online class camera or in an ID photo and it’s right back.\n\nIt’s the left side—my nose and jaw just look off to me, crooked. And even when people say they don’t see it, I’m in the CNC lab thinking they’re noticing that before anything I’m doing. Like before my work or whether I actually know what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your face is actually fine and you're just perceiving it differently?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe, I guess it’s possible, but it doesn’t feel that way when I look at myself. Like I came in because I can’t stop thinking about the left side of my face looking off—my nose and jaw, especially in ID photos or on the camera during online classes.\n\nIt’s like when I’m in the CNC lab and a part is just a little misaligned, even if someone else says it’s fine, I keep seeing the offset. And with my face it’s… I don’t know, people tell me they don’t see it, but I still feel like everyone notices that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people can see that your face is crooked, even if they don't say anything about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I know they don’t usually say anything, and if I ask they’ll say they don’t see it, but I still feel like they can. Like the left side of my face, my nose and jaw, it looks crooked to me in every ID photo or when I’m on the online class camera.\n\nAnd then in the CNC lab, I’ll be standing there trying to focus on the work, and I’m thinking they’re probably noticing my face first before they notice whether the part is right or if my program is good. It’s hard to explain, but it feels really obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that others are intentionally not commenting on your face because they don't want to hurt your feelings, rather than them actually noticing something wrong?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of what I think sometimes. Like maybe they don’t want to be rude or make me feel worse, so they just say they don’t see it.\n\nBut I came in because I can’t stop thinking about it — the left side of my face just looks off to me, especially my nose and jaw. It’s in every ID photo, and on the online class camera it’s like I can’t stop staring at it. And then I’ll be in the CNC lab trying to focus on a setup or a program, and instead I’m thinking everyone’s noticing my face before they notice my work.\n\nIt’s hard because even if they’re trying to be nice, it still feels like they can see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your face is actually normal and that your perception of it being crooked is not based on how it really looks?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe there’s a possibility, yeah. I can say that sitting here. But then when I actually see myself, like in an ID photo or on the online class camera, it just doesn’t feel normal. The left side looks off to me—my nose and jaw look crooked—and I can’t stop seeing it.\n\nIt’s kind of like in the CNC lab when a setup looks slightly wrong and I keep checking it even if someone else says it’s within tolerance… sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked. But yeah, people tell me they don’t see it, and I try to believe them, but I still feel like everyone is noticing my face before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Does your idea of how crooked your face looks ever change, or is it always the same?\n\nPatient: It changes a little, I guess… like some days it looks worse depending on the photo or the camera angle. But it’s always kind of there. I came in because I just can’t stop thinking about the left side of my face looking off, like my nose and jaw are crooked.\n\nIt’s weird because in ID photos or on the online class camera, I’ll keep staring at it, and then even in the CNC lab I’m supposed to be thinking about the machine or the program, and my mind goes to, “They’re noticing my face before they notice my work.” Even when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like they do.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the appearance of your face affected your relationships or daily activities in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, more than I probably want to admit. I’ve missed CNC labs and some shop shifts because I just couldn’t get myself out the door. I’d be ready, or almost ready, and then I’d catch my face in the mirror or think about the lights in the shop and everyone seeing me, and then suddenly I’m just stuck.\n\nAnd now my faculty mentor said my work placement could be in jeopardy, which… I know sounds serious. It is serious. I keep thinking I should be focusing on programming and setups and all that, because that’s what I’m actually there for, but instead I’m worried they’re looking at my face.\n\nI also stopped playing soccer and volunteering. I used to like that stuff, especially soccer, but now it feels like too many people looking at me from all angles, so I just don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social situations because you're worried about how people might see your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I’ve stopped going to a lot of things. Like soccer, I just don’t play anymore, and I used to actually like it. Same with volunteering—I kind of just dropped it because I don’t want people seeing my face from every angle.\n\nAnd it’s not just social stuff, I guess. I’ve missed CNC labs and shop shifts too because I’ll be trying to leave and then I get stuck thinking about how I look, and I can’t make myself go. My mentor even said my work placement could be in jeopardy, which makes me feel worse, but then that almost makes it harder to show up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact on your daily life or overall well-being due to your concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s kind of taken over more than I thought it would. Like, I’ve missed CNC labs and shop shifts because I’ll get ready and then just can’t get myself out the door. And now my faculty mentor said my work placement might be in jeopardy, which is… I mean, that’s a big deal.\n\nAnd then outside of school or work, I stopped playing soccer and volunteering. I used to like having something to do, you know, like being part of something, but now I just think about people looking at me, especially from the side, and I end up not going. It’s annoying because I know I should be worried about normal stuff, like finishing my program, but it always comes back to my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked is based on reality or is it something that you just can't shake no matter what others tell you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I want to say it’s based on reality because I can see it. Like in every ID photo, or when the online class camera flips or doesn’t flip, I don’t even know which one is worse, but the left side of my face just looks off. My nose and jaw look crooked to me.\n\nBut then people say they don’t see it, or they act like I’m making a big deal out of nothing, and I don’t know… I still can’t shake it. I’ll be in the CNC lab trying to focus on the setup or whatever, and I just feel like everyone notices my face before they notice anything I’m doing. So even if they say they don’t see it, it doesn’t really make it go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked would change if you saw a picture of yourself that someone else had taken and they told you you looked fine in it?\n\nPatient: I mean… probably not, honestly. I’d want it to, but if someone else took the picture and said I looked fine, I’d still zoom in or compare sides or think maybe it was just the angle.\n\nIt’s like, I came in because I can’t stop thinking the left side of my face looks off—my nose, my jaw, all of it. It shows up to me in ID photos, and then with online class cameras I get stuck wondering if it’s flipped or not flipped, and then I’m thinking about that instead of the class. Even in the CNC lab, I feel like people notice my face before they notice my work, even if they tell me they don’t see anything. So… yeah, one picture probably wouldn’t really change it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could convince you that your face is not actually crooked?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I keep thinking maybe if there was some perfect angle or like, I don’t know, a scan or something, but then I’d probably still find a way to question it.\n\nBecause I came in because I just can’t stop thinking the left side of my face looks off. Like my nose and jaw look crooked to me in ID photos, and then with the online class camera I get stuck on whether it’s flipped or not, and I’ll be thinking about that instead of what we’re supposed to be doing. Even in the CNC lab, I feel like people notice my face before they notice my work.\n\nSo if people just say, “No, you look fine,” it doesn’t really convince me. I wish it did, but it just doesn’t stick.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   {

Like in every ID photo, or when the online class camera flips or doesn’t flip, I don’t even know which one is worse, but the left side of my face just looks off. My nose and jaw look crooked to me.\n\nBut then people say they don’t see it, or they act like I’m making a big deal out of nothing, and I don’t know… I still can’t shake it. I’ll be in the CNC lab trying to focus on the setup or whatever, and I just feel like everyone notices my face before they notice anything I’m doing. So even if they say they don’t see it, it doesn’t really make it go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked would change if you saw a picture of yourself that someone else had taken and they told you you looked fine in it?\n\nPatient: I mean… probably not, honestly. I’d want it to, but if someone else took the picture and said I looked fine, I’d still zoom in or compare sides or think maybe it was just the angle.\n\nIt’s like, I came in because I can’t stop thinking the left side of my face looks off—my nose, my jaw, all of it. It shows up to me in ID photos, and then with online class cameras I get stuck wondering if it’s flipped or not flipped, and then I’m thinking about that instead of the class. Even in the CNC lab, I feel like people notice my face before they notice my work, even if they tell me they don’t see anything. So… yeah, one picture probably wouldn’t really change it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could convince you that your face is not actually crooked?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I keep thinking maybe if there was some perfect angle or like, I don’t know, a scan or something, but then I’d probably still find a way to question it.\n\nBecause I came in because I just can’t stop thinking the left side of my face looks off. Like my nose and jaw look crooked to me in ID photos, and then with the online class camera I get stuck on whether it’s flipped or not, and I’ll be thinking about that instead of what we’re supposed to be doing. Even in the CNC lab, I feel like people notice my face before they notice my work.\n\nSo if people just say, “No, you look fine,” it doesn’t really convince me. I wish it did, but it just doesn’t stick.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your belief about your face being crooked is something that might not be true, even if you're completely convinced of it right now?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Maybe… I mean, I guess it might not be true, because people keep telling me they don’t see it. But when I look at myself, especially in ID photos or on the online class camera, it’s like the left side of my face just jumps out at me as wrong—my nose, my jaw, everything looks crooked.

And then I’ll be in the CNC lab and I’m supposed to be thinking about the work, the setup, measurements, whatever, but I’m thinking, like, are they looking at my face before they’re looking at what I’m doing? So I can say “maybe it’s not true,” but it doesn’t feel that way in the moment. It feels completely obvious to me.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, I’m here because I can’t really stop thinking about my face. Like, especially the left side. It looks wrong to me—my nose, my jaw, like they’re crooked or shifted or something.\n\nIt shows up really bad in ID photos, and on the camera for online classes too. I know cameras distort things sometimes—like in the CNC lab we were talking about calibration and how tiny offsets can mess up a whole cut, and I keep thinking it’s like that with my face, like something is just off by a little but everyone can see it.\n\nPeople tell me they don’t notice anything, but I don’t really believe them. I feel like when I’m in the lab, people notice that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually is crooked, or is it just that you feel like it looks that way to you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I think it actually is crooked. Or at least, it looks so off to me that it’s hard to believe it’s just in my head. Like the left side of my face—my nose and my jaw—especially in ID photos or on the camera for online classes, it’s like it jumps out at me.\n\nAnd I know people say they don’t see it, but then I’m in the CNC lab and I’m trying to focus on the machine or the program, and I just feel like everyone’s noticing my face before they notice anything I’m doing. Like the work doesn’t even matter because that’s the first thing they see.\n\nDoctor: Does your concern about the left side of your face looking crooked stay the same all the time, or are there times when it bothers you less?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty much there all the time, I guess. Like, maybe I can get distracted for a bit if I’m really focused on a program or setting up a machine, but then I’ll see myself on the online class camera or in an ID photo and it’s right back.\n\nIt’s the left side—my nose and jaw just look off to me, crooked. And even when people say they don’t see it, I’m in the CNC lab thinking they’re noticing that before anything I’m doing. Like before my work or whether I actually know what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your face is actually fine and you're just perceiving it differently?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe, I guess it’s possible, but it doesn’t feel that way when I look at myself. Like I came in because I can’t stop thinking about the left side of my face looking off—my nose and jaw, especially in ID photos or on the camera during online classes.\n\nIt’s like when I’m in the CNC lab and a part is just a little misaligned, even if someone else says it’s fine, I keep seeing the offset. And with my face it’s… I don’t know, people tell me they don’t see it, but I still feel like everyone notices that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people can see that your face is crooked, even if they don't say anything about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I know they don’t usually say anything, and if I ask they’ll say they don’t see it, but I still feel like they can. Like the left side of my face, my nose and jaw, it looks crooked to me in every ID photo or when I’m on the online class camera.\n\nAnd then in the CNC lab, I’ll be standing there trying to focus on the work, and I’m thinking they’re probably noticing my face first before they notice whether the part is right or if my program is good. It’s hard to explain, but it feels really obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that others are intentionally not commenting on your face because they don't want to hurt your feelings, rather than them actually noticing something wrong?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of what I think sometimes. Like maybe they don’t want to be rude or make me feel worse, so they just say they don’t see it.\n\nBut I came in because I can’t stop thinking about it — the left side of my face just looks off to me, especially my nose and jaw. It’s in every ID photo, and on the online class camera it’s like I can’t stop staring at it. And then I’ll be in the CNC lab trying to focus on a setup or a program, and instead I’m thinking everyone’s noticing my face before they notice my work.\n\nIt’s hard because even if they’re trying to be nice, it still feels like they can see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your face is actually normal and that your perception of it being crooked is not based on how it really looks?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe there’s a possibility, yeah. I can say that sitting here. But then when I actually see myself, like in an ID photo or on the online class camera, it just doesn’t feel normal. The left side looks off to me—my nose and jaw look crooked—and I can’t stop seeing it.\n\nIt’s kind of like in the CNC lab when a setup looks slightly wrong and I keep checking it even if someone else says it’s within tolerance… sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked. But yeah, people tell me they don’t see it, and I try to believe them, but I still feel like everyone is noticing my face before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Does your idea of how crooked your face looks ever change, or is it always the same?\n\nPatient: It changes a little, I guess… like some days it looks worse depending on the photo or the camera angle. But it’s always kind of there. I came in because I just can’t stop thinking about the left side of my face looking off, like my nose and jaw are crooked.\n\nIt’s weird because in ID photos or on the online class camera, I’ll keep staring at it, and then even in the CNC lab I’m supposed to be thinking about the machine or the program, and my mind goes to, “They’re noticing my face before they notice my work.” Even when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like they do.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the appearance of your face affected your relationships or daily activities in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, more than I probably want to admit. I’ve missed CNC labs and some shop shifts because I just couldn’t get myself out the door. I’d be ready, or almost ready, and then I’d catch my face in the mirror or think about the lights in the shop and everyone seeing me, and then suddenly I’m just stuck.\n\nAnd now my faculty mentor said my work placement could be in jeopardy, which… I know sounds serious. It is serious. I keep thinking I should be focusing on programming and setups and all that, because that’s what I’m actually there for, but instead I’m worried they’re looking at my face.\n\nI also stopped playing soccer and volunteering. I used to like that stuff, especially soccer, but now it feels like too many people looking at me from all angles, so I just don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social situations because you're worried about how people might see your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I’ve stopped going to a lot of things. Like soccer, I just don’t play anymore, and I used to actually like it. Same with volunteering—I kind of just dropped it because I don’t want people seeing my face from every angle.\n\nAnd it’s not just social stuff, I guess. I’ve missed CNC labs and shop shifts too because I’ll be trying to leave and then I get stuck thinking about how I look, and I can’t make myself go. My mentor even said my work placement could be in jeopardy, which makes me feel worse, but then that almost makes it harder to show up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact on your daily life or overall well-being due to your concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s kind of taken over more than I thought it would. Like, I’ve missed CNC labs and shop shifts because I’ll get ready and then just can’t get myself out the door. And now my faculty mentor said my work placement might be in jeopardy, which is… I mean, that’s a big deal.\n\nAnd then outside of school or work, I stopped playing soccer and volunteering. I used to like having something to do, you know, like being part of something, but now I just think about people looking at me, especially from the side, and I end up not going. It’s annoying because I know I should be worried about normal stuff, like finishing my program, but it always comes back to my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked is based on reality or is it something that you just can't shake no matter what others tell you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I want to say it’s based on reality because I can see it. Like in every ID photo, or when the online class camera flips or doesn’t flip, I don’t even know which one is worse, but the left side of my face just looks off. My nose and jaw look crooked to me.\n\nBut then people say they don’t see it, or they act like I’m making a big deal out of nothing, and I don’t know… I still can’t shake it. I’ll be in the CNC lab trying to focus on the setup or whatever, and I just feel like everyone notices my face before they notice anything I’m doing. So even if they say they don’t see it, it doesn’t really make it go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked would change if you saw a picture of yourself that someone else had taken and they told you you looked fine in it?\n\nPatient: I mean… probably not, honestly. I’d want it to, but if someone else took the picture and said I looked fine, I’d still zoom in or compare sides or think maybe it was just the angle.\n\nIt’s like, I came in because I can’t stop thinking the left side of my face looks off—my nose, my jaw, all of it. It shows up to me in ID photos, and then with online class cameras I get stuck wondering if it’s flipped or not flipped, and then I’m thinking about that instead of the class. Even in the CNC lab, I feel like people notice my face before they notice my work, even if they tell me they don’t see anything. So… yeah, one picture probably wouldn’t really change it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could convince you that your face is not actually crooked?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I keep thinking maybe if there was some perfect angle or like, I don’t know, a scan or something, but then I’d probably still find a way to question it.\n\nBecause I came in because I just can’t stop thinking the left side of my face looks off. Like my nose and jaw look crooked to me in ID photos, and then with the online class camera I get stuck on whether it’s flipped or not, and I’ll be thinking about that instead of what we’re supposed to be doing. Even in the CNC lab, I feel like people notice my face before they notice my work.\n\nSo if people just say, “No, you look fine,” it doesn’t really convince me. I wish it did, but it just doesn’t stick.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked is something that might not be true, even if you're completely convinced of it right now?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I mean, I guess it might not be true, because people keep telling me they don’t see it. But when I look at myself, especially in ID photos or on the online class camera, it’s like the left side of my face just jumps out at me as wrong—my nose, my jaw, everything looks crooked.\n\nAnd then I’ll be in the CNC lab and I’m supposed to be thinking about the work, the setup, measurements, whatever, but I’m thinking, like, are they looking at my face before they’re looking at what I’m doing? So I can say “maybe it’s not true,” but it doesn’t feel that way in the moment. It feels completely obvious to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I mean, I know they don’t usually say anything, and if I ask they’ll say they don’t see it, but I still feel like they can. Like the left side of my face, my nose and jaw, it looks crooked to me in every ID photo or when I’m on the online class camera.\n\nAnd then in the CNC lab, I’ll be standing there trying to focus on the work, and I’m thinking they’re probably noticing my face first before they notice whether the part is right or if my program is good. It’s hard to explain, but it feels really obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that others are intentionally not commenting on your face because they don't want to hurt your feelings, rather than them actually noticing something wrong?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of what I think sometimes. Like maybe they don’t want to be rude or make me feel worse, so they just say they don’t see it.\n\nBut I came in because I can’t stop thinking about it — the left side of my face just looks off to me, especially my nose and jaw. It’s in every ID photo, and on the online class camera it’s like I can’t stop staring at it. And then I’ll be in the CNC lab trying to focus on a setup or a program, and instead I’m thinking everyone’s noticing my face before they notice my work.\n\nIt’s hard because even if they’re trying to be nice, it still feels like they can see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your face is actually normal and that your perception of it being crooked is not based on how it really looks?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe there’s a possibility, yeah. I can say that sitting here. But then when I actually see myself, like in an ID photo or on the online class camera, it just doesn’t feel normal. The left side looks off to me—my nose and jaw look crooked—and I can’t stop seeing it.\n\nIt’s kind of like in the CNC lab when a setup looks slightly wrong and I keep checking it even if someone else says it’s within tolerance… sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked. But yeah, people tell me they don’t see it, and I try to believe them, but I still feel like everyone is noticing my face before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Does your idea of how crooked your face looks ever change, or is it always the same?\n\nPatient: It changes a little, I guess… like some days it looks worse depending on the photo or the camera angle. But it’s always kind of there. I came in because I just can’t stop thinking about the left side of my face looking off, like my nose and jaw are crooked.\n\nIt’s weird because in ID photos or on the online class camera, I’ll keep staring at it, and then even in the CNC lab I’m supposed to be thinking about the machine or the program, and my mind goes to, “They’re noticing my face before they notice my work.” Even when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like they do.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the appearance of your face affected your relationships or daily activities in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, more than I probably want to admit. I’ve missed CNC labs and some shop shifts because I just couldn’t get myself out the door. I’d be ready, or almost ready, and then I’d catch my face in the mirror or think about the lights in the shop and everyone seeing me, and then suddenly I’m just stuck.\n\nAnd now my faculty mentor said my work placement could be in jeopardy, which… I know sounds serious. It is serious. I keep thinking I should be focusing on programming and setups and all that, because that’s what I’m actually there for, but instead I’m worried they’re looking at my face.\n\nI also stopped playing soccer and volunteering. I used to like that stuff, especially soccer, but now it feels like too many people looking at me from all angles, so I just don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social situations because you're worried about how people might see your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I’ve stopped going to a lot of things. Like soccer, I just don’t play anymore, and I used to actually like it. Same with volunteering—I kind of just dropped it because I don’t want people seeing my face from every angle.\n\nAnd it’s not just social stuff, I guess. I’ve missed CNC labs and shop shifts too because I’ll be trying to leave and then I get stuck thinking about how I look, and I can’t make myself go. My mentor even said my work placement could be in jeopardy, which makes me feel worse, but then that almost makes it harder to show up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact on your daily life or overall well-being due to your concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s kind of taken over more than I thought it would. Like, I’ve missed CNC labs and shop shifts because I’ll get ready and then just can’t get myself out the door. And now my faculty mentor said my work placement might be in jeopardy, which is… I mean, that’s a big deal.\n\nAnd then outside of school or work, I stopped playing soccer and volunteering. I used to like having something to do, you know, like being part of something, but now I just think about people looking at me, especially from the side, and I end up not going. It’s annoying because I know I should be worried about normal stuff, like finishing my program, but it always comes back to my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked is based on reality or is it something that you just can't shake no matter what others tell you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I want to say it’s based on reality because I can see it. Like in every ID photo, or when the online class camera flips or doesn’t flip, I don’t even know which one is worse, but the left side of my face just looks off. My nose and jaw look crooked to me.\n\nBut then people say they don’t see it, or they act like I’m making a big deal out of nothing, and I don’t know… I still can’t shake it. I’ll be in the CNC lab trying to focus on the setup or whatever, and I just feel like everyone notices my face before they notice anything I’m doing. So even if they say they don’t see it, it doesn’t really make it go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked would change if you saw a picture of yourself that someone else had taken and they told you you looked fine in it?\n\nPatient: I mean… probably not, honestly. I’d want it to, but if someone else took the picture and said I looked fine, I’d still zoom in or compare sides or think maybe it was just the angle.\n\nIt’s like, I came in because I can’t stop thinking the left side of my face looks off—my nose, my jaw, all of it. It shows up to me in ID photos, and then with online class cameras I get stuck wondering if it’s flipped or not flipped, and then I’m thinking about that instead of the class. Even in the CNC lab, I feel like people notice my face before they notice my work, even if they tell me they don’t see anything. So… yeah, one picture probably wouldn’t really change it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could convince you that your face is not actually crooked?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I keep thinking maybe if there was some perfect angle or like, I don’t know, a scan or something, but then I’d probably still find a way to question it.\n\nBecause I came in because I just can’t stop thinking the left side of my face looks off. Like my nose and jaw look crooked to me in ID photos, and then with the online class camera I get stuck on whether it’s flipped or not, and I’ll be thinking about that instead of what we’re supposed to be doing. Even in the CNC lab, I feel like people notice my face before they notice my work.\n\nSo if people just say, “No, you look fine,” it doesn’t really convince me. I wish it did, but it just doesn’t stick.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked is something that might not be true, even if you're completely convinced of it right now?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I mean, I guess it might not be true, because people keep telling me they don’t see it. But when I look at myself, especially in ID photos or on the online class camera, it’s like the left side of my face just jumps out at me as wrong—my nose, my jaw, everything looks crooked.\n\nAnd then I’ll be in the CNC lab and I’m supposed to be thinking about the work, the setup, measurements, whatever, but I’m thinking, like, are they looking at my face before they’re looking at what I’m doing? So I can say “maybe it’s not true,” but it doesn’t feel that way in the moment. It feels completely obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your perception of your face being crooked is a result of your own thoughts and not based on reality?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe. I can say that’s possible, like logically, because people keep telling me they don’t see it. But when I’m actually looking at my face, it doesn’t feel like just my thoughts. It feels like the left side is obviously off—my nose, my jaw, especially in ID photos or on the online class camera.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about it everywhere, like in the CNC lab I’m supposed to be focused on the work, but I’m wondering if everyone is noticing my face before they notice what I’m doing. So… yeah, maybe it’s in my perception, but it really feels real to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look normal to you if everyone around you, including experts, told you it was perfectly fine?\n\nPatient: I want to say yes, but I don’t think it would actually work like that. Like, even if everyone said it was fine—even an expert—I’d probably still get stuck on it when I see an ID photo or the online class camera. The left side just looks off to me, like my nose and jaw are crooked.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about stupid stuff like lighting, or whether the camera is flipped, or how in the CNC lab people are probably noticing my face before they notice anything I’m doing. So… maybe for a minute I’d feel better, but I don’t think it would look normal to me for long.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… maybe it would change for a little bit if a doctor or plastic surgeon said that, but I don’t think it would really go away. I came in because I can’t stop thinking about it—like the left side of my face just looks off to me, my nose and jaw look crooked, especially in ID photos or on the online class camera.

And it’s weird because I know people say they don’t see it, but then I’m at the CNC lab and I’m supposed to be focused on the machine or the measurements, and instead I’m thinking they’re noticing my face before they notice my work. So if a surgeon said it was fine, I’d probably try to believe them, but I’d still end up checking a picture or seeing myself on camera and thinking, no, it’s there.
